Well, I’m here because things at work have gotten… not normal. I do mathematical modeling, environmental datasets, contract work, and there’s this project where I’m supposed to be leading the revisions. And I know people keep saying it’s just version-control problems, you know, files getting overwritten or someone using an older copy, but it’s more patterned than that.

It looks to me like the people on that environmental modeling contract are tracking my revisions. I’ll make changes, careful changes, and then errors show up in the datasets later—errors I know I didn’t put there. And they’re the kind of mistakes that make me look sloppy, like I can’t manage the work or shouldn’t be leading it. My wife says I’m reading too much into it, and a couple of the junior analysts act like it’s just normal confusion with shared files, but… I’ve done this kind of work a long time. I don’t know if I’d put it exactly like “ordinary things” sending me messages. It’s not like the television is talking to me or road signs are giving me instructions or anything like that.

But with the work files, yes, there are things that feel very specifically aimed at me. Like when I make revisions on the environmental modeling contract, and then later errors appear in the datasets—errors that are just the kind that would make me look incompetent as the lead. To someone else, maybe it looks like a normal version-control issue, someone used the wrong file or overwrote something. My wife says that, and the junior analysts say that too. But the pattern is too consistent.

It feels like those mistakes are not random. They’re placed there to send a message, I guess—not in words, but to show me that people are tracking what I’m changing and can interfere with it. And also to make other people think I can’t handle the project. So… not special messages from objects, no. But the changes in the datasets, the timing of them, the way they show up after I’ve touched the work—that feels meant for me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
If someone showed me clear proof… I mean, I’d have to look at what you mean by “clear,” because that’s the problem. People keep saying “version-control mistakes” like that explains everything, but it doesn’t explain the timing, or why the errors show up right after I’ve made revisions, or why they’re the kind of errors that make me look like I don’t know what I’m doing.

If there was real proof, not just someone saying, “Oh, this happens all the time,” I’d consider it. I’m not trying to be unreasonable. I have a math background, I understand mistakes happen in files and datasets. But this has felt too consistent to just wave away. My wife says I’m reading too much into it, and the junior analysts say it’s probably routine file confusion, but from where I’m sitting it looks like people on that contract are tracking my changes and planting problems so I’ll take the blame.

So… would I still believe it? I think I’d still be suspicious, yes. Maybe I’d question it a little if the proof was really solid, but I don’t think one explanation would erase the whole pattern for me.

At least, I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll replay conversations in my head, especially work conversations, like what someone said in a meeting or how they said it, and I can get pretty stuck on that. But that’s more like thinking through it, not a voice coming from outside me. So… no, I wouldn’t say I’m hearing voices.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually energized or high lately?\n\nPatient: Unusually energized or high? No, I don’t think so. Not “high,” anyway. I haven’t been walking around feeling euphoric or like I have limitless energy, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I can get keyed up when I’m trying to sort something out, especially if there’s a deadline or if something in the work doesn’t add up. Then I’ll keep turning it over in my head because I don’t like leaving a problem unresolved. But that’s not the same as feeling great or energized in some unusual way. It feels more like… pressure, or being on alert. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve been unusually energized.\n\nDoctor: Do ordinary things around you ever seem to carry a special message meant specifically for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly like “ordinary things” sending me messages. It’s not like the television is talking to me or road signs are giving me instructions or anything like that.\n\nBut with the work files, yes, there are things that feel very specifically aimed at me. Like when I make revisions on the environmental modeling contract, and then later errors appear in the datasets—errors that are just the kind that would make me look incompetent as the lead. To someone else, maybe it looks like a normal version-control issue, someone used the wrong file or overwrote something. My wife says that, and the junior analysts say that too. But the pattern is too consistent.\n\nIt feels like those mistakes are not random. They’re placed there to send a message, I guess—not in words, but to show me that people are tracking what I’m changing and can interfere with it. And also to make other people think I can’t handle the project. So… not special messages from objects, no. But the changes in the datasets, the timing of them, the way they show up after I’ve touched the work—that feels meant for me.\n\nDoctor: If someone showed you clear proof that these file errors were just routine version-control mistakes, would you still believe people were deliberately trying to undermine you?\n\nPatient: If someone showed me clear proof… I mean, I’d have to look at what you mean by “clear,” because that’s the problem. People keep saying “version-control mistakes” like that explains everything, but it doesn’t explain the timing, or why the errors show up right after I’ve made revisions, or why they’re the kind of errors that make me look like I don’t know what I’m doing.\n\nIf there was real proof, not just someone saying, “Oh, this happens all the time,” I’d consider it. I’m not trying to be unreasonable. I have a math background, I understand mistakes happen in files and datasets. But this has felt too consistent to just wave away. My wife says I’m reading too much into it, and the junior analysts say it’s probably routine file confusion, but from where I’m sitting it looks like people on that contract are tracking my changes and planting problems so I’ll take the blame.\n\nSo… would I still believe it? I think I’d still be suspicious, yes. Maybe I’d question it a little if the proof was really solid, but I don’t think one explanation would erase the whole pattern for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I wouldn’t say it’s been my whole adult life. I mean, I’ve always been careful with my work, maybe more careful than some people, because in math and modeling a small error can make you look bad even if the rest of the work is solid. But this particular sense—that people on this project are watching my revisions and then errors are showing up afterward, like they’re being placed there—has really been more recent.

I’d say about the last two months. That’s when it started feeling less like ordinary sloppiness or file confusion and more like a pattern aimed at me. Before that, I might have been irritated by mistakes or office politics like anyone else, but I wasn’t walking around thinking people were tracking my work this way. It’s really this project, and the last couple of months, where it became hard to ignore.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d describe it exactly as my face or voice being flat. People don’t usually say it to me that way, at least not directly. But I have noticed that I’m… less responsive, I guess. Less animated.

These days I can sit for hours at my desk, or out in the garage, and the telescope will still be covered. I know I used to like using it. I used to go to those astronomy nights, talk with people about equipment or what we were looking at, and it wasn’t a chore. Same with hiking, or even mentoring the younger analysts at work—I used to get something out of explaining a problem or helping someone see the structure of it.

Now I’ll think, “I should do that,” or “I used to care about that,” but there’s just not much pull to start. And talking to people feels like it takes more effort than it should. So maybe that comes across as flat. I’m not trying to be cold or anything. It’s more like things don’t get enough traction inside me to show on the outside.

Then I’ll keep turning it over in my head because I don’t like leaving a problem unresolved. But that’s not the same as feeling great or energized in some unusual way. Yes… yes, it has. I don’t like saying that, because my work has always been the one area where I felt pretty competent, but compared with a few months ago I’m not keeping up the way I used to.

I’ve fallen behind on the modeling deliverables—far enough that it wasn’t just me staying late to catch up or asking for an extra day. The firm had to reassign pieces of the contract to other people. That’s embarrassing. And it’s not just the technical work itself; I’m supposed to be supervising some of the junior analysts, helping them think through assumptions and check their approach, and lately I’m not much use to them. I either don’t respond quickly, or I get stuck in the weeds and can’t give them the kind of clear guidance I used to.

At home it’s similar, I guess. I mostly keep to myself, and my wife has to push me to keep basic routines going—getting moving, taking care of ordinary things. So, yes, noticeably worse. Not completely unable to work, but definitely not functioning at the level I was.

I know what ordinary mistakes look like. This feels planted.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices when no one else is there?\n\nPatient: Voices? I mean… no, not in the way people usually mean that, I don’t think. I’m not sitting in a room hearing someone talking to me when nobody’s there. At least, I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll replay conversations in my head, especially work conversations, like what someone said in a meeting or how they said it, and I can get pretty stuck on that. But that’s more like thinking through it, not a voice coming from outside me. So… no, I wouldn’t say I’m hearing voices.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually energized or high lately?\n\nPatient: Unusually energized or high? No, I don’t think so. Not “high,” anyway. I haven’t been walking around feeling euphoric or like I have limitless energy, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I can get keyed up when I’m trying to sort something out, especially if there’s a deadline or if something in the work doesn’t add up. Then I’ll keep turning it over in my head because I don’t like leaving a problem unresolved. But that’s not the same as feeling great or energized in some unusual way. It feels more like… pressure, or being on alert. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve been unusually energized.\n\nDoctor: Do ordinary things around you ever seem to carry a special message meant specifically for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly like “ordinary things” sending me messages. It’s not like the television is talking to me or road signs are giving me instructions or anything like that.\n\nBut with the work files, yes, there are things that feel very specifically aimed at me. Like when I make revisions on the environmental modeling contract, and then later errors appear in the datasets—errors that are just the kind that would make me look incompetent as the lead. To someone else, maybe it looks like a normal version-control issue, someone used the wrong file or overwrote something. My wife says that, and the junior analysts say that too. But the pattern is too consistent.\n\nIt feels like those mistakes are not random. They’re placed there to send a message, I guess—not in words, but to show me that people are tracking what I’m changing and can interfere with it. And also to make other people think I can’t handle the project. So… not special messages from objects, no. But the changes in the datasets, the timing of them, the way they show up after I’ve touched the work—that feels meant for me.\n\nDoctor: If someone showed you clear proof that these file errors were just routine version-control mistakes, would you still believe people were deliberately trying to undermine you?\n\nPatient: If someone showed me clear proof… I mean, I’d have to look at what you mean by “clear,” because that’s the problem. People keep saying “version-control mistakes” like that explains everything, but it doesn’t explain the timing, or why the errors show up right after I’ve made revisions, or why they’re the kind of errors that make me look like I don’t know what I’m doing.\n\nIf there was real proof, not just someone saying, “Oh, this happens all the time,” I’d consider it. I’m not trying to be unreasonable. I have a math background, I understand mistakes happen in files and datasets. But this has felt too consistent to just wave away. My wife says I’m reading too much into it, and the junior analysts say it’s probably routine file confusion, but from where I’m sitting it looks like people on that contract are tracking my changes and planting problems so I’ll take the blame.\n\nSo… would I still believe it? I think I’d still be suspicious, yes. Maybe I’d question it a little if the proof was really solid, but I don’t think one explanation would erase the whole pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed most of the day nearly every day… I’m not sure I’d say that. I mean, I’ve had times where I feel worn down or frustrated, especially when things feel like they’re not going right, but “depressed” sounds like a very specific thing, like feeling low all day, day after day.\n\nI don’t think I’ve noticed that in myself in that way. I can get discouraged, sure, and I can spend a lot of time thinking about things, but I wouldn’t say I’m sad or depressed most of the day nearly every day. Same with hiking, or even mentoring the younger analysts at work—I used to get something out of explaining a problem or helping someone see the structure of it.\n\nNow I’ll think, “I should do that,” or “I used to care about that,” but there’s just not much pull to start. It’s more like things don’t get enough traction inside me to show on the outside.\n\nDoctor: Compared with a few months ago, has your ability to do your job gotten noticeably worse?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I don’t like saying that, because my work has always been the one area where I felt pretty competent, but compared with a few months ago I’m not keeping up the way I used to.\n\nI’ve fallen behind on the modeling deliverables—far enough that it wasn’t just me staying late to catch up or asking for an extra day. The firm had to reassign pieces of the contract to other people. That’s embarrassing. And it’s not just the technical work itself; I’m supposed to be supervising some of the junior analysts, helping them think through assumptions and check their approach, and lately I’m not much use to them. I either don’t respond quickly, or I get stuck in the weeds and can’t give them the kind of clear guidance I used to.\n\nAt home it’s similar, I guess. I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think people have said it in exactly those words, like “you’re hard to follow” or “you’re jumping around.” At least, not that I remember.

Sometimes my wife will ask me to slow down or explain what I mean, but that could just be because I’m giving too much context. I’ve always had a tendency to do that—if I’m explaining a work problem, I want to start with the assumptions, then the model, then why one change affects another thing. So I don’t always know whether someone is confused because I’m actually not making sense, or because I’m taking the long way around.

At work, people have seemed impatient with me lately, or they’ll sort of steer the conversation back to the main point. But I don’t know that anyone has directly told me my speech doesn’t make sense. I haven’t noticed myself suddenly switching topics for no reason, if that’s what you mean. I may go into too much detail, but in my mind there’s usually a connection.

At least, I haven’t noticed anything like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll replay conversations in my head, especially work conversations, like what someone said in a meeting or how they said it, and I can get pretty stuck on that. But that’s more like thinking through it, not a voice coming from outside me. So… no, I wouldn’t say I’m hearing voices.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling unusually energized or high lately?\n\nPatient: Unusually energized or high? No, I don’t think so. Not “high,” anyway. I haven’t been walking around feeling euphoric or like I have limitless energy, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I can get keyed up when I’m trying to sort something out, especially if there’s a deadline or if something in the work doesn’t add up. Then I’ll keep turning it over in my head because I don’t like leaving a problem unresolved. But that’s not the same as feeling great or energized in some unusual way. It feels more like… pressure, or being on alert. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve been unusually energized.\n\nDoctor: Do ordinary things around you ever seem to carry a special message meant specifically for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it exactly like “ordinary things” sending me messages. It’s not like the television is talking to me or road signs are giving me instructions or anything like that.\n\nBut with the work files, yes, there are things that feel very specifically aimed at me. Like when I make revisions on the environmental modeling contract, and then later errors appear in the datasets—errors that are just the kind that would make me look incompetent as the lead. To someone else, maybe it looks like a normal version-control issue, someone used the wrong file or overwrote something. My wife says that, and the junior analysts say that too. But the pattern is too consistent.\n\nIt feels like those mistakes are not random. They’re placed there to send a message, I guess—not in words, but to show me that people are tracking what I’m changing and can interfere with it. And also to make other people think I can’t handle the project. So… not special messages from objects, no. But the changes in the datasets, the timing of them, the way they show up after I’ve touched the work—that feels meant for me.\n\nDoctor: If someone showed you clear proof that these file errors were just routine version-control mistakes, would you still believe people were deliberately trying to undermine you?\n\nPatient: If someone showed me clear proof… I mean, I’d have to look at what you mean by “clear,” because that’s the problem. People keep saying “version-control mistakes” like that explains everything, but it doesn’t explain the timing, or why the errors show up right after I’ve made revisions, or why they’re the kind of errors that make me look like I don’t know what I’m doing.\n\nIf there was real proof, not just someone saying, “Oh, this happens all the time,” I’d consider it. I’m not trying to be unreasonable. I have a math background, I understand mistakes happen in files and datasets. But this has felt too consistent to just wave away. My wife says I’m reading too much into it, and the junior analysts say it’s probably routine file confusion, but from where I’m sitting it looks like people on that contract are tracking my changes and planting problems so I’ll take the blame.\n\nSo… would I still believe it? I think I’d still be suspicious, yes. Maybe I’d question it a little if the proof was really solid, but I don’t think one explanation would erase the whole pattern for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Depressed most of the day nearly every day… I’m not sure I’d say that. I mean, I’ve had times where I feel worn down or frustrated, especially when things feel like they’re not going right, but “depressed” sounds like a very specific thing, like feeling low all day, day after day.\n\nI don’t think I’ve noticed that in myself in that way. I can get discouraged, sure, and I can spend a lot of time thinking about things, but I wouldn’t say I’m sad or depressed most of the day nearly every day. At least not that I’d describe it that way.\n\nDoctor: Did this sense that people are tracking your work and trying to undermine you start recently, or have you tended to feel this way about people for much of your adult life?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t say it’s been my whole adult life. I mean, I’ve always been careful with my work, maybe more careful than some people, because in math and modeling a small error can make you look bad even if the rest of the work is solid. But this particular sense—that people on this project are watching my revisions and then errors are showing up afterward, like they’re being placed there—has really been more recent.\n\nI’d say about the last two months. That’s when it started feeling less like ordinary sloppiness or file confusion and more like a pattern aimed at me. Before that, I might have been irritated by mistakes or office politics like anyone else, but I wasn’t walking around thinking people were tracking my work this way. It’s really this project, and the last couple of months, where it became hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Has this belief that people are deliberately altering your work been present continuously for at least one month?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, if you’re asking whether it’s just been a passing thought for a few days, then no—it’s been there longer than that. The feeling that people on the project are watching my revisions and then deliberately putting errors in, that’s been going on for about the last two months.\n\nIt hasn’t been, you know, every single second of every day in the sense that I can’t think about anything else, but the belief itself hasn’t really gone away. Once I started seeing that pattern, it’s been hard to unsee it. So yes, definitely more than a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you become much less emotionally expressive lately—for example, do people say your face or voice seems flat?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d describe it exactly as my face or voice being flat. People don’t usually say it to me that way, at least not directly. But I have noticed that I’m… less responsive, I guess. Less animated.\n\nThese days I can sit for hours at my desk, or out in the garage, and the telescope will still be covered. I know I used to like using it. I used to go to those astronomy nights, talk with people about equipment or what we were looking at, and it wasn’t a chore. Same with hiking, or even mentoring the younger analysts at work—I used to get something out of explaining a problem or helping someone see the structure of it.\n\nNow I’ll think, “I should do that,” or “I used to care about that,” but there’s just not much pull to start. And talking to people feels like it takes more effort than it should. So maybe that comes across as flat. I’m not trying to be cold or anything. It’s more like things don’t get enough traction inside me to show on the outside.\n\nDoctor: Compared with a few months ago, has your ability to do your job gotten noticeably worse?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I don’t like saying that, because my work has always been the one area where I felt pretty competent, but compared with a few months ago I’m not keeping up the way I used to.\n\nI’ve fallen behind on the modeling deliverables—far enough that it wasn’t just me staying late to catch up or asking for an extra day. The firm had to reassign pieces of the contract to other people. That’s embarrassing. And it’s not just the technical work itself; I’m supposed to be supervising some of the junior analysts, helping them think through assumptions and check their approach, and lately I’m not much use to them. I either don’t respond quickly, or I get stuck in the weeds and can’t give them the kind of clear guidance I used to.\n\nAt home it’s similar, I guess. I mostly keep to myself, and my wife has to push me to keep basic routines going—getting moving, taking care of ordinary things. So, yes, noticeably worse. Not completely unable to work, but definitely not functioning at the level I was.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you lately that your speech is hard to follow or that you jump from one idea to another in a way that doesn’t make sense to them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think people have said it in exactly those words, like “you’re hard to follow” or “you’re jumping around.” At least, not that I remember.\n\nSometimes my wife will ask me to slow down or explain what I mean, but that could just be because I’m giving too much context. I’ve always had a tendency to do that—if I’m explaining a work problem, I want to start with the assumptions, then the model, then why one change affects another thing. So I don’t always know whether someone is confused because I’m actually not making sense, or because I’m taking the long way around.\n\nAt work, people have seemed impatient with me lately, or they’ll sort of steer the conversation back to the main point. But I don’t know that anyone has directly told me my speech doesn’t make sense. I haven’t noticed myself suddenly switching topics for no reason, if that’s what you mean. I may go into too much detail, but in my mind there’s usually a connection.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes. It’s definitely been more than six months.

If I try to put a real starting point on it, I’d say the whole decline started after I was put in charge of that larger environmental modeling contract… that was about two years and two months ago now. Before that, I might have been irritated by mistakes or office politics like anyone else, but I wasn’t walking around thinking people were tracking my work this way. I mean, if you’re asking whether it’s just been a passing thought for a few days, then no—it’s been there longer than that. The feeling that people on the project are watching my revisions and then deliberately putting errors in, that’s been going on for about the last two months.\n\nIt hasn’t been, you know, every single second of every day in the sense that I can’t think about anything else, but the belief itself hasn’t really gone away. Once I started seeing that pattern, it’s been hard to unsee it. So yes, definitely more than a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you become much less emotionally expressive lately—for example, do people say your face or voice seems flat?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d describe it exactly as my face or voice being flat. People don’t usually say it to me that way, at least not directly. But I have noticed that I’m… less responsive, I guess. Less animated.\n\nThese days I can sit for hours at my desk, or out in the garage, and the telescope will still be covered. I know I used to like using it. I used to go to those astronomy nights, talk with people about equipment or what we were looking at, and it wasn’t a chore. Same with hiking, or even mentoring the younger analysts at work—I used to get something out of explaining a problem or helping someone see the structure of it.\n\nNow I’ll think, “I should do that,” or “I used to care about that,” but there’s just not much pull to start. And talking to people feels like it takes more effort than it should. So maybe that comes across as flat. I’m not trying to be cold or anything. It’s more like things don’t get enough traction inside me to show on the outside.\n\nDoctor: Compared with a few months ago, has your ability to do your job gotten noticeably worse?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I don’t like saying that, because my work has always been the one area where I felt pretty competent, but compared with a few months ago I’m not keeping up the way I used to.\n\nI’ve fallen behind on the modeling deliverables—far enough that it wasn’t just me staying late to catch up or asking for an extra day. The firm had to reassign pieces of the contract to other people. That’s embarrassing. And it’s not just the technical work itself; I’m supposed to be supervising some of the junior analysts, helping them think through assumptions and check their approach, and lately I’m not much use to them. I either don’t respond quickly, or I get stuck in the weeds and can’t give them the kind of clear guidance I used to.\n\nAt home it’s similar, I guess. I mostly keep to myself, and my wife has to push me to keep basic routines going—getting moving, taking care of ordinary things. So, yes, noticeably worse. Not completely unable to work, but definitely not functioning at the level I was.\n\nDoctor: Have other people told you lately that your speech is hard to follow or that you jump from one idea to another in a way that doesn’t make sense to them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think people have said it in exactly those words, like “you’re hard to follow” or “you’re jumping around.” At least, not that I remember.\n\nSometimes my wife will ask me to slow down or explain what I mean, but that could just be because I’m giving too much context. I’ve always had a tendency to do that—if I’m explaining a work problem, I want to start with the assumptions, then the model, then why one change affects another thing. So I don’t always know whether someone is confused because I’m actually not making sense, or because I’m taking the long way around.\n\nAt work, people have seemed impatient with me lately, or they’ll sort of steer the conversation back to the main point. But I don’t know that anyone has directly told me my speech doesn’t make sense. I haven’t noticed myself suddenly switching topics for no reason, if that’s what you mean. I may go into too much detail, but in my mind there’s usually a connection.\n\nDoctor: Have these problems overall been going on for six months or more?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s definitely been more than six months.\n\nIf I try to put a real starting point on it, I’d say the whole decline started after I was put in charge of that larger environmental modeling contract… that was about two years and two months ago now. At first I kept telling myself it was just the pressure of the project, or that I was adjusting to the responsibility, because it was a bigger scope than what I’d handled before. But looking back, that’s when things began shifting, and it hasn’t really gone back to normal since then.\n\nSo, yes—this isn’t just a few bad weeks or even a rough season. It’s been going on for quite a while.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food). Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy. Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nUnusually energized or high? No, I don’t think so. Not “high,” anyway. I haven’t been walking around feeling euphoric or like I have limitless energy, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI mean, I can get keyed up when I’m trying to sort something out, especially if there’s a deadline or if something in the work doesn’t add up. Then I’ll keep turning it over in my head because I don’t like leaving a problem unresolved. But that’s not the same as feeling great or energized in some unusual way. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve been unusually energized.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }

Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don’t know if I’d put it exactly like “ordinary things” sending me messages. It’s not like the television is talking to me or road signs are giving me instructions or anything like that.\n\nBut with the work files, yes, there are things that feel very specifically aimed at me. Like when I make revisions on the environmental modeling contract, and then later errors appear in the datasets—errors that are just the kind that would make me look incompetent as the lead. To someone else, maybe it looks like a normal version-control issue, someone used the wrong file or overwrote something. My wife says that, and the junior analysts say that too. But the pattern is too consistent.\n\nIt feels like those mistakes are not random. They’re placed there to send a message, I guess—not in words, but to show me that people are tracking what I’m changing and can interfere with it. And also to make other people think I can’t handle the project. So… not special messages from objects, no. But the changes in the datasets, the timing of them, the way they show up after I’ve touched the work—that feels meant for me.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nDepressed most of the day nearly every day… I’m not sure I’d say that. I mean, I’ve had times where I feel worn down or frustrated, especially when things feel like they’re not going right, but “depressed” sounds like a very specific thing, like feeling low all day, day after day.\n\nI don’t think I’ve noticed that in myself in that way. I can get discouraged, sure, and I can spend a lot of time thinking about things, but I wouldn’t say I’m sad or depressed most of the day nearly every day. At least not that I’d describe it that way.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"functional_impairment\",\"name\":\"Functional Impairment\",\"description\":\"required\",\"reason\":\"The doctor is asking whether the symptoms have noticeably worsened his occupational functioning, which directly matches functional impairment at work.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. I’ve always had a tendency to do that—if I’m explaining a work problem, I want to start with the assumptions, then the model, then why one change affects another thing. So I don’t always know whether someone is confused because I’m actually not making sense, or because I’m taking the long way around.\n\nAt work, people have seemed impatient with me lately, or they’ll sort of steer the conversation back to the main point.
